[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here mostly because my family and a few people around me keep saying I’ve been doing too much. But honestly, from where I’m standing, it doesn’t feel like “too much” so much as finally getting things moving the way they ought to be moved.\n\nWe’ve got this move to handle, and if I don’t keep after every box, every list, every little detail, it just sits there. And then there’s the new garage — I don’t want it thrown together sloppy, I want it rebuilt right, with a proper setup, the way a man can actually use it. On top of that I’ve been helping organize things at church, because somebody has to keep people on track there too. And even at work, I still try to show the younger fellows how the job ought to be done, because they rush or they miss steps, and I’ve been around long enough to know better.\n\nSo I guess that’s why I’m here. Everybody says I’m pushing too hard, taking on too much, but to me it feels like I’m the only one with enough drive to get it all handled.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're the only one who really knows how to get things done properly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, if I’m being honest. I don’t mean that to sound arrogant, but it’s just… when something needs doing, I can see all the pieces of it. Other people seem to look at one box or one errand or one little task and think that’s enough, but I’m looking at the whole thing.\n\nWith the move, for example, there are boxes, labels, what goes where, what has to be done first, what can’t be left until the last minute — and if I don’t keep pushing, it just drifts. Same with the garage. I don’t want some half-done setup where tools are piled in a corner and nothing makes sense. I want it rebuilt properly, laid out right, usable.\n\nAnd church too — people mean well, but somebody has to organize things or everyone just talks in circles. Then at work, I still try to show the younger fellows the right way, because they cut corners or don’t think ahead. So yes, I suppose I do feel like I’m the one with enough drive to actually get things done the way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your efforts to get everything done are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Stress or anxiety… I don’t know that I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I get worked up when things aren’t being handled right, sure. Anybody would, if they saw things sliding or people standing around waiting for someone else to make a decision.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel to me like worry, like I’m sitting there scared or panicking. It’s more like there’s a lot to do and I can see it all, so I keep moving. Maybe other people look at that and say I’m stressed, but from my side it feels more like pressure to keep everything from turning into a mess.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it as anxiety. More like frustration when folks don’t keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things have to be done exactly your way in order for them to be done correctly?\n\nPatient: Well… “exactly my way” sounds a little harsher than I’d put it, but yes, I suppose there’s truth in that. It feels like I can already see how something ought to be done, start to finish, and when people start questioning every step or trying to do it some sideways way, I get… I get pretty impatient.\n\nLately it’s been more than just wanting things orderly. I’ll be in a great mood, joking around, feeling like I’ve got all the energy in the world, like I’m ten feet tall and can carry the whole thing myself. And then somebody — my wife, or my boy — asks one little question, something simple like why I’m doing it that way or whether it can wait, and I snap. I don’t always mean to, but it comes out like, “Either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of my way.”\n\nBecause in that moment it doesn’t feel like they’re helping. It feels like they’re slowing me down. And I know that probably sounds difficult to live with, but when I’m in it, I just feel so sure that my plan is the right one and everybody else needs to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Are your relationships with family or friends being affected by your behavior or the way you're handling things lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like admitting that, because in my head I’m just trying to keep things moving and make sure nobody drops the ball, but yes, it’s affected things.\n\nAt work, it got bad enough that they pulled me off after a safety incident. I was sure I had things under control, and I didn’t see it the way they saw it at the time, but afterward everybody acted like I couldn’t be trusted to make the right call. That was humiliating. I’ve worked hard my whole life, and then suddenly people are looking at me like I’m a problem.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and family have definitely changed how they deal with me. They don’t really trust me with certain decisions right now — money, driving, even parts of the move. They’ll say they’re just being careful, or that things have gotten out of hand, but it feels like they’re taking over. If I want to make a purchase or decide when we’re going somewhere or how the move should be handled, there’s this pause, this look between them, like they’ve already decided I’m not being reasonable.\n\nSo yes, my relationships are strained. My wife is tired, my family is wary around me, and I can feel that. I still think a lot of what I was trying to do needed doing, but I can’t really say it hasn’t caused trouble, because it has.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over, even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: Repeating thoughts or behaviors… I’m not sure I know exactly what you mean by that.\n\nI don’t think I have, like, a ritual or something I’m doing over and over because I can’t stop myself. I’m not counting things or checking a lock twenty times, if that’s what you’re asking. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nNow, I do get stuck on things in the sense that if something needs doing, I’ll keep coming back to it in my head, and I’ll talk about it more than other people probably want me to. My wife might say I repeat myself, because I’ll keep explaining why something has to be handled a certain way. But from my side, that feels more like trying to get the point across, not like some thought I’m trying to stop and can’t.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing, unless I’m misunderstanding the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of handling things is causing you more problems than it's solving?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to admit that it is. I don’t like saying that, because when I’m doing it, it feels like I’m solving the problem. It feels like I’m the one moving things along while everybody else is standing around talking it to death. But then afterward there’s a bill, or an argument, or somebody upset with me, and I can see it’s made more mess than it fixed.\n\nLike with the garage work and some equipment — I went ahead and put deposits down without really sitting down with my wife and talking it through. In my mind, I was thinking, “If I don’t move on this now, we’ll miss the chance, or the work won’t get done, or it’ll cost more later.” It felt practical to me at the time, even responsible in a way. But I didn’t stop and think how it would feel to her, finding out money had already been committed and she hadn’t been part of the decision. So instead of getting something handled, I created a whole other problem at home.\n\nAnd the same thing happened with the driving back and forth between the houses. People were telling me I was doing too much, that I was taking chances, and I heard them, but I didn’t really take it in. I kept thinking, “It has to get done, and I’m the one who can do it.” Same at the plant — I was rushing jobs, pushing through, even after people warned me I was cutting it too close or taking risks. I thought I was being efficient. Looking back, I can see how it looked reckless.\n\nSo yes, if I’m being honest, my way of handling things has caused more problems than I meant it to. I still have this feeling like things won’t get done right unless I stay on top of them, but I can’t deny that I’ve caused trouble by charging ahead.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your attention to details and desire for things to be done a certain way interferes with your ability to complete tasks efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly.\n\nI mean, I do like things done correctly. I’ve worked around machinery and jobs where if somebody skips a step or does it sloppy, it can matter. So I’ve always paid attention to how things are done, and I can get irritated if people act like “close enough” is good enough. But I don’t think I’m sitting there polishing one tiny detail so long that nothing gets finished.\n\nIf anything, lately people would probably say I’m going too fast, not too slow. My problem hasn’t felt like getting stuck on details — it’s more that I see what needs doing and I want it handled, and I don’t have much patience for waiting around or rehashing it over and over.\n\nNow, maybe my wife would say I have a certain way I want things done and I push that too hard. I can see that. If I think a job should be done in a particular order, I’ll keep explaining it, and I may not leave much room for somebody else’s way. But in my mind that’s about being efficient, not being unable to finish.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that attention to details is keeping me from completing tasks efficiently. If there’s interference, it’s probably more in how I deal with people when I think something needs to get done a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior or the way you've been handling things lately is significantly impacting your daily life or relationships in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, it has. I wish I could say it was just people overreacting or not understanding what I was trying to do, but it’s gotten to the point where there have been real consequences.\n\nAt work, I ended up being pulled off after a safety incident. That’s not a small thing. I’ve worked around equipment for years, so being told I couldn’t keep going because of how I was handling things — that hit me hard. In my head I thought I was just moving fast and getting things done, but other people saw it as unsafe, and apparently it was serious enough that they took me out of it.\n\nAnd at home it’s affected just about everything. My wife and family don’t really trust me right now with decisions — money decisions, driving back and forth, things about the move. They’ve started stepping in or questioning me because they feel like things got out of hand. I don’t like being treated like I can’t make my own choices, but I also can’t pretend they’re making it up. I did make decisions without talking them through, and I pushed ahead when people were telling me to slow down.\n\nSo yes, it’s impacted my daily life and relationships. It’s caused arguments, it’s made my family nervous around my judgment, and it’s affected my work. That’s hard for me to admit, but it’s true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things would fall apart or not meet your standards if you weren't in charge of them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do feel that way. I know that probably sounds arrogant, and I don’t mean it that way. It just feels like if I step back, things won’t be handled the way they should be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My problem hasn’t felt like getting stuck on details — it’s more that I see what needs doing and I want it handled, and I don’t have much patience for waiting around or rehashing it over and over.\n\nNow, maybe my wife would say I have a certain way I want things done and I push that too hard. I can see that. If I think a job should be done in a particular order, I’ll keep explaining it, and I may not leave much room for somebody else’s way. But in my mind that’s about being efficient, not being unable to finish.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that attention to details is keeping me from completing tasks efficiently. If there’s interference, it’s probably more in how I deal with people when I think something needs to get done a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior or the way you've been handling things lately is significantly impacting your daily life or relationships in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, it has. I wish I could say it was just people overreacting or not understanding what I was trying to do, but it’s gotten to the point where there have been real consequences.\n\nAt work, I ended up being pulled off after a safety incident. That’s not a small thing. I’ve worked around equipment for years, so being told I couldn’t keep going because of how I was handling things — that hit me hard. In my head I thought I was just moving fast and getting things done, but other people saw it as unsafe, and apparently it was serious enough that they took me out of it.\n\nAnd at home it’s affected just about everything. My wife and family don’t really trust me right now with decisions — money decisions, driving back and forth, things about the move. They’ve started stepping in or questioning me because they feel like things got out of hand. I don’t like being treated like I can’t make my own choices, but I also can’t pretend they’re making it up. I did make decisions without talking them through, and I pushed ahead when people were telling me to slow down.\n\nSo yes, it’s impacted my daily life and relationships. It’s caused arguments, it’s made my family nervous around my judgment, and it’s affected my work. That’s hard for me to admit, but it’s true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things would fall apart or not meet your standards if you weren't in charge of them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do feel that way. Maybe more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up thinking, “I have to control everything.” I don’t see it that way in the moment. What I feel is that there are all these things that need doing, and if I don’t push them forward, they’re either not going to happen, or they’re going to be done halfway. The move, for example — there’s just so much involved, and I keep thinking, “Somebody has to keep this moving.” And with the new garage, I want it rebuilt right, not patched together or delayed until everyone forgets what needed to be done.\n\nAnd it’s not only at home. At church, if things are disorganized, I start seeing what needs to be arranged and who should be doing what, and I feel like I can help get it straightened out. Then at work, with the younger fellows, I find myself wanting to show them how the job ought to be done, because I’ve been doing this kind of work a long time and I know when someone is cutting corners or not thinking ahead.\n\nSo yes, I’m here because everyone says I’ve been doing too much, but from inside my own head it feels more like I’m the only one with enough drive to keep all these things from slipping. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Perfect? No… I don’t think I’d use that word.

I mean, there are things I want done properly. If something is supposed to be level, safe, lined up, measured right — then yes, I don’t like calling it finished if it’s obviously wrong. That’s just how I was brought up and how I worked. In the kind of work I’ve done, you can’t just say, “Well, it’s mostly okay,” if mostly okay means somebody gets hurt or the machine doesn’t run right.

But “perfect” sounds like I’m standing there unable to move on because one little thing is off, and I don’t really see myself that way. I’m not saying every screw has to shine or every corner has to look brand new before I’ll accept it. I can live with things being good enough if they’re actually good enough.

What bothers me is when people say something is done and I can tell they rushed it or skipped parts, or they didn’t think through what happens next. Then I have a hard time letting it go. I’ll keep pointing it out, and maybe I push too much, because in my mind I’m thinking, “If we’re doing it, why not do it right the first time?”

So… no, not perfect exactly. But I do have a pretty firm idea of what “done right” means, and I don’t always trust other people’s version of “done.”

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here mostly because my family and a few people around me keep saying I’ve been doing too much. But honestly, from where I’m standing, it doesn’t feel like “too much” so much as finally getting things moving the way they ought to be moved.\n\nWe’ve got this move to handle, and if I don’t keep after every box, every list, every little detail, it just sits there. And then there’s the new garage — I don’t want it thrown together sloppy, I want it rebuilt right, with a proper setup, the way a man can actually use it. On top of that I’ve been helping organize things at church, because somebody has to keep people on track there too. And even at work, I still try to show the younger fellows how the job ought to be done, because they rush or they miss steps, and I’ve been around long enough to know better.\n\nSo I guess that’s why I’m here. Everybody says I’m pushing too hard, taking on too much, but to me it feels like I’m the only one with enough drive to get it all handled.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're the only one who really knows how to get things done properly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, if I’m being honest. I don’t mean that to sound arrogant, but it’s just… when something needs doing, I can see all the pieces of it. Other people seem to look at one box or one errand or one little task and think that’s enough, but I’m looking at the whole thing.\n\nWith the move, for example, there are boxes, labels, what goes where, what has to be done first, what can’t be left until the last minute — and if I don’t keep pushing, it just drifts. Same with the garage. I don’t want some half-done setup where tools are piled in a corner and nothing makes sense. I want it rebuilt properly, laid out right, usable.\n\nAnd church too — people mean well, but somebody has to organize things or everyone just talks in circles. Then at work, I still try to show the younger fellows the right way, because they cut corners or don’t think ahead. So yes, I suppose I do feel like I’m the one with enough drive to actually get things done the way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your efforts to get everything done are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Stress or anxiety… I don’t know that I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I get worked up when things aren’t being handled right, sure. Anybody would, if they saw things sliding or people standing around waiting for someone else to make a decision.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel to me like worry, like I’m sitting there scared or panicking. It’s more like there’s a lot to do and I can see it all, so I keep moving. Maybe other people look at that and say I’m stressed, but from my side it feels more like pressure to keep everything from turning into a mess.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it as anxiety. More like frustration when folks don’t keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things have to be done exactly your way in order for them to be done correctly?\n\nPatient: Well… “exactly my way” sounds a little harsher than I’d put it, but yes, I suppose there’s truth in that. It feels like I can already see how something ought to be done, start to finish, and when people start questioning every step or trying to do it some sideways way, I get… I get pretty impatient.\n\nLately it’s been more than just wanting things orderly. I’ll be in a great mood, joking around, feeling like I’ve got all the energy in the world, like I’m ten feet tall and can carry the whole thing myself. And then somebody — my wife, or my boy — asks one little question, something simple like why I’m doing it that way or whether it can wait, and I snap. I don’t always mean to, but it comes out like, “Either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of my way.”\n\nBecause in that moment it doesn’t feel like they’re helping. It feels like they’re slowing me down. And I know that probably sounds difficult to live with, but when I’m in it, I just feel so sure that my plan is the right one and everybody else needs to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Are your relationships with family or friends being affected by your behavior or the way you're handling things lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like admitting that, because in my head I’m just trying to keep things moving and make sure nobody drops the ball, but yes, it’s affected things.\n\nAt work, it got bad enough that they pulled me off after a safety incident. I was sure I had things under control, and I didn’t see it the way they saw it at the time, but afterward everybody acted like I couldn’t be trusted to make the right call. That was humiliating. I’ve worked hard my whole life, and then suddenly people are looking at me like I’m a problem.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and family have definitely changed how they deal with me. They don’t really trust me with certain decisions right now — money, driving, even parts of the move. They’ll say they’re just being careful, or that things have gotten out of hand, but it feels like they’re taking over. If I want to make a purchase or decide when we’re going somewhere or how the move should be handled, there’s this pause, this look between them, like they’ve already decided I’m not being reasonable.\n\nSo yes, my relationships are strained. My wife is tired, my family is wary around me, and I can feel that. I still think a lot of what I was trying to do needed doing, but I can’t really say it hasn’t caused trouble, because it has.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over, even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: Repeating thoughts or behaviors… I’m not sure I know exactly what you mean by that.\n\nI don’t think I have, like, a ritual or something I’m doing over and over because I can’t stop myself. I’m not counting things or checking a lock twenty times, if that’s what you’re asking. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nNow, I do get stuck on things in the sense that if something needs doing, I’ll keep coming back to it in my head, and I’ll talk about it more than other people probably want me to. My wife might say I repeat myself, because I’ll keep explaining why something has to be handled a certain way. But from my side, that feels more like trying to get the point across, not like some thought I’m trying to stop and can’t.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing, unless I’m misunderstanding the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of handling things is causing you more problems than it's solving?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to admit that it is. I don’t like saying that, because when I’m doing it, it feels like I’m solving the problem. It feels like I’m the one moving things along while everybody else is standing around talking it to death. But then afterward there’s a bill, or an argument, or somebody upset with me, and I can see it’s made more mess than it fixed.\n\nLike with the garage work and some equipment — I went ahead and put deposits down without really sitting down with my wife and talking it through. In my mind, I was thinking, “If I don’t move on this now, we’ll miss the chance, or the work won’t get done, or it’ll cost more later.” It felt practical to me at the time, even responsible in a way. But I didn’t stop and think how it would feel to her, finding out money had already been committed and she hadn’t been part of the decision. So instead of getting something handled, I created a whole other problem at home.\n\nAnd the same thing happened with the driving back and forth between the houses. People were telling me I was doing too much, that I was taking chances, and I heard them, but I didn’t really take it in. I kept thinking, “It has to get done, and I’m the one who can do it.” Same at the plant — I was rushing jobs, pushing through, even after people warned me I was cutting it too close or taking risks. I thought I was being efficient. Looking back, I can see how it looked reckless.\n\nSo yes, if I’m being honest, my way of handling things has caused more problems than I meant it to. I still have this feeling like things won’t get done right unless I stay on top of them, but I can’t deny that I’ve caused trouble by charging ahead.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your attention to details and desire for things to be done a certain way interferes with your ability to complete tasks efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly.\n\nI mean, I do like things done correctly. I’ve worked around machinery and jobs where if somebody skips a step or does it sloppy, it can matter. So I’ve always paid attention to how things are done, and I can get irritated if people act like “close enough” is good enough. But I don’t think I’m sitting there polishing one tiny detail so long that nothing gets finished.\n\nIf anything, lately people would probably say I’m going too fast, not too slow. My problem hasn’t felt like getting stuck on details — it’s more that I see what needs doing and I want it handled, and I don’t have much patience for waiting around or rehashing it over and over.\n\nNow, maybe my wife would say I have a certain way I want things done and I push that too hard. I can see that. If I think a job should be done in a particular order, I’ll keep explaining it, and I may not leave much room for somebody else’s way. But in my mind that’s about being efficient, not being unable to finish.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that attention to details is keeping me from completing tasks efficiently. If there’s interference, it’s probably more in how I deal with people when I think something needs to get done a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior or the way you've been handling things lately is significantly impacting your daily life or relationships in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, it has. I wish I could say it was just people overreacting or not understanding what I was trying to do, but it’s gotten to the point where there have been real consequences.\n\nAt work, I ended up being pulled off after a safety incident. That’s not a small thing. I’ve worked around equipment for years, so being told I couldn’t keep going because of how I was handling things — that hit me hard. In my head I thought I was just moving fast and getting things done, but other people saw it as unsafe, and apparently it was serious enough that they took me out of it.\n\nAnd at home it’s affected just about everything. My wife and family don’t really trust me right now with decisions — money decisions, driving back and forth, things about the move. They’ve started stepping in or questioning me because they feel like things got out of hand. I don’t like being treated like I can’t make my own choices, but I also can’t pretend they’re making it up. I did make decisions without talking them through, and I pushed ahead when people were telling me to slow down.\n\nSo yes, it’s impacted my daily life and relationships. It’s caused arguments, it’s made my family nervous around my judgment, and it’s affected my work. That’s hard for me to admit, but it’s true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things would fall apart or not meet your standards if you weren't in charge of them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do feel that way. Maybe more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up thinking, “I have to control everything.” I don’t see it that way in the moment. What I feel is that there are all these things that need doing, and if I don’t push them forward, they’re either not going to happen, or they’re going to be done halfway. The move, for example — there’s just so much involved, and I keep thinking, “Somebody has to keep this moving.” And with the new garage, I want it rebuilt right, not patched together or delayed until everyone forgets what needed to be done.\n\nAnd it’s not only at home. At church, if things are disorganized, I start seeing what needs to be arranged and who should be doing what, and I feel like I can help get it straightened out. Then at work, with the younger fellows, I find myself wanting to show them how the job ought to be done, because I’ve been doing this kind of work a long time and I know when someone is cutting corners or not thinking ahead.\n\nSo yes, I’m here because everyone says I’ve been doing too much, but from inside my own head it feels more like I’m the only one with enough drive to keep all these things from slipping. I know that probably sounds arrogant, and I don’t mean it that way. It just feels like if I step back, things won’t be handled the way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things have to be perfect, or as close to perfect as possible, for you to consider them done?\n\nPatient: Perfect? No… I don’t think I’d use that word.\n\nI mean, there are things I want done properly. If something is supposed to be level, safe, lined up, measured right — then yes, I don’t like calling it finished if it’s obviously wrong. That’s just how I was brought up and how I worked. In the kind of work I’ve done, you can’t just say, “Well, it’s mostly okay,” if mostly okay means somebody gets hurt or the machine doesn’t run right.\n\nBut “perfect” sounds like I’m standing there unable to move on because one little thing is off, and I don’t really see myself that way. I’m not saying every screw has to shine or every corner has to look brand new before I’ll accept it. I can live with things being good enough if they’re actually good enough.\n\nWhat bothers me is when people say something is done and I can tell they rushed it or skipped parts, or they didn’t think through what happens next. Then I have a hard time letting it go. I’ll keep pointing it out, and maybe I push too much, because in my mind I’m thinking, “If we’re doing it, why not do it right the first time?”\n\nSo… no, not perfect exactly. But I do have a pretty firm idea of what “done right” means, and I don’t always trust other people’s version of “done.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And then there’s the new garage — I don’t want it thrown together sloppy, I want it rebuilt right, with a proper setup, the way a man can actually use it. On top of that I’ve been helping organize things at church, because somebody has to keep people on track there too. I mean, I get worked up when things aren’t being handled right, sure. I mean… yes, it has. That’s not a small thing. I’ve worked around equipment for years, so being told I couldn’t keep going because of how I was handling things — that hit me hard. In my head I thought I was just moving fast and getting things done, but other people saw it as unsafe, and apparently it was serious enough that they took me out of it.\n\nAnd at home it’s affected just about everything. My wife and family don’t really trust me right now with decisions — money decisions, driving back and forth, things about the move. I did make decisions without talking them through, and I pushed ahead when people were telling me to slow down.\n\nSo yes, it’s impacted my daily life and relationships. It’s caused arguments, it’s made my family nervous around my judgment, and it’s affected my work. That’s hard for me to admit, but it’s true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things would fall apart or not meet your standards if you weren't in charge of them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do feel that way. Maybe more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up thinking, “I have to control everything.” I don’t see it that way in the moment. What I feel is that there are all these things that need doing, and if I don’t push them forward, they’re either not going to happen, or they’re going to be done halfway. The move, for example — there’s just so much involved, and I keep thinking, “Somebody has to keep this moving.” And with the new garage, I want it rebuilt right, not patched together or delayed until everyone forgets what needed to be done.\n\nAnd it’s not only at home. At church, if things are disorganized, I start seeing what needs to be arranged and who should be doing what, and I feel like I can help get it straightened out. Then at work, with the younger fellows, I find myself wanting to show them how the job ought to be done, because I’ve been doing this kind of work a long time and I know when someone is cutting corners or not thinking ahead.\n\nSo yes, I’m here because everyone says I’ve been doing too much, but from inside my own head it feels more like I’m the only one with enough drive to keep all these things from slipping. I know that probably sounds arrogant, and I don’t mean it that way. It just feels like if I step back, things won’t be handled the way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things have to be perfect, or as close to perfect as possible, for you to consider them done?\n\nPatient: Perfect? No… I don’t think I’d use that word.\n\nI mean, there are things I want done properly. If something is supposed to be level, safe, lined up, measured right — then yes, I don’t like calling it finished if it’s obviously wrong. That’s just how I was brought up and how I worked. In the kind of work I’ve done, you can’t just say, “Well, it’s mostly okay,” if mostly okay means somebody gets hurt or the machine doesn’t run right.\n\nBut “perfect” sounds like I’m standing there unable to move on because one little thing is off, and I don’t really see myself that way. I’m not saying every screw has to shine or every corner has to look brand new before I’ll accept it. I can live with things being good enough if they’re actually good enough.\n\nWhat bothers me is when people say something is done and I can tell they rushed it or skipped parts, or they didn’t think through what happens next. Then I have a hard time letting it go. I’ll keep pointing it out, and maybe I push too much, because in my mind I’m thinking, “If we’re doing it, why not do it right the first time?”\n\nSo… no, not perfect exactly. But I do have a pretty firm idea of what “done right” means, and I don’t always trust other people’s version of “done.”\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel like you're superior to others or have a stronger sense of self-importance than those around you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / crushing_grinding_polishing_mixing_or_blending_worker / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d say “superior.” That word makes me uncomfortable, honestly. I don’t walk around thinking I’m better than everybody else as a person, or that I’m more important in some grand way.

But… I can see how I might come across like that. Especially when I’m convinced I know the right way something should be handled. I’ve got a lot of years behind me, I’ve done hard work, I’ve had to learn by doing, and sometimes when someone younger or less experienced is making a decision, I catch myself thinking, “No, no, that’s not right — you’re missing the obvious thing here.” And I probably don’t always say that gently.

So if you mean, do I sometimes believe my judgment is better than other people’s in certain situations? Yes, probably. I do. I don’t think that’s the same as feeling superior overall, but maybe the people around me don’t feel the difference when I’m pushing my opinion. They just hear me acting like I know best.

I guess I’d put it this way: I don’t feel like I’m above people, but I do have trouble stepping back when I think I understand something better than they do. And maybe that can look like self-importance from the outside.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here mostly because my family and a few people around me keep saying I’ve been doing too much. But honestly, from where I’m standing, it doesn’t feel like “too much” so much as finally getting things moving the way they ought to be moved.\n\nWe’ve got this move to handle, and if I don’t keep after every box, every list, every little detail, it just sits there. And then there’s the new garage — I don’t want it thrown together sloppy, I want it rebuilt right, with a proper setup, the way a man can actually use it. On top of that I’ve been helping organize things at church, because somebody has to keep people on track there too. And even at work, I still try to show the younger fellows how the job ought to be done, because they rush or they miss steps, and I’ve been around long enough to know better.\n\nSo I guess that’s why I’m here. Everybody says I’m pushing too hard, taking on too much, but to me it feels like I’m the only one with enough drive to get it all handled.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're the only one who really knows how to get things done properly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, if I’m being honest. I don’t mean that to sound arrogant, but it’s just… when something needs doing, I can see all the pieces of it. Other people seem to look at one box or one errand or one little task and think that’s enough, but I’m looking at the whole thing.\n\nWith the move, for example, there are boxes, labels, what goes where, what has to be done first, what can’t be left until the last minute — and if I don’t keep pushing, it just drifts. Same with the garage. I don’t want some half-done setup where tools are piled in a corner and nothing makes sense. I want it rebuilt properly, laid out right, usable.\n\nAnd church too — people mean well, but somebody has to organize things or everyone just talks in circles. Then at work, I still try to show the younger fellows the right way, because they cut corners or don’t think ahead. So yes, I suppose I do feel like I’m the one with enough drive to actually get things done the way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your efforts to get everything done are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Stress or anxiety… I don’t know that I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I get worked up when things aren’t being handled right, sure. Anybody would, if they saw things sliding or people standing around waiting for someone else to make a decision.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel to me like worry, like I’m sitting there scared or panicking. It’s more like there’s a lot to do and I can see it all, so I keep moving. Maybe other people look at that and say I’m stressed, but from my side it feels more like pressure to keep everything from turning into a mess.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it as anxiety. More like frustration when folks don’t keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things have to be done exactly your way in order for them to be done correctly?\n\nPatient: Well… “exactly my way” sounds a little harsher than I’d put it, but yes, I suppose there’s truth in that. It feels like I can already see how something ought to be done, start to finish, and when people start questioning every step or trying to do it some sideways way, I get… I get pretty impatient.\n\nLately it’s been more than just wanting things orderly. I’ll be in a great mood, joking around, feeling like I’ve got all the energy in the world, like I’m ten feet tall and can carry the whole thing myself. And then somebody — my wife, or my boy — asks one little question, something simple like why I’m doing it that way or whether it can wait, and I snap. I don’t always mean to, but it comes out like, “Either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of my way.”\n\nBecause in that moment it doesn’t feel like they’re helping. It feels like they’re slowing me down. And I know that probably sounds difficult to live with, but when I’m in it, I just feel so sure that my plan is the right one and everybody else needs to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Are your relationships with family or friends being affected by your behavior or the way you're handling things lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like admitting that, because in my head I’m just trying to keep things moving and make sure nobody drops the ball, but yes, it’s affected things.\n\nAt work, it got bad enough that they pulled me off after a safety incident. I was sure I had things under control, and I didn’t see it the way they saw it at the time, but afterward everybody acted like I couldn’t be trusted to make the right call. That was humiliating. I’ve worked hard my whole life, and then suddenly people are looking at me like I’m a problem.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and family have definitely changed how they deal with me. They don’t really trust me with certain decisions right now — money, driving, even parts of the move. They’ll say they’re just being careful, or that things have gotten out of hand, but it feels like they’re taking over. If I want to make a purchase or decide when we’re going somewhere or how the move should be handled, there’s this pause, this look between them, like they’ve already decided I’m not being reasonable.\n\nSo yes, my relationships are strained. My wife is tired, my family is wary around me, and I can feel that. I still think a lot of what I was trying to do needed doing, but I can’t really say it hasn’t caused trouble, because it has.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over, even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: Repeating thoughts or behaviors… I’m not sure I know exactly what you mean by that.\n\nI don’t think I have, like, a ritual or something I’m doing over and over because I can’t stop myself. I’m not counting things or checking a lock twenty times, if that’s what you’re asking. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nNow, I do get stuck on things in the sense that if something needs doing, I’ll keep coming back to it in my head, and I’ll talk about it more than other people probably want me to. My wife might say I repeat myself, because I’ll keep explaining why something has to be handled a certain way. But from my side, that feels more like trying to get the point across, not like some thought I’m trying to stop and can’t.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing, unless I’m misunderstanding the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of handling things is causing you more problems than it's solving?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to admit that it is. I don’t like saying that, because when I’m doing it, it feels like I’m solving the problem. It feels like I’m the one moving things along while everybody else is standing around talking it to death. But then afterward there’s a bill, or an argument, or somebody upset with me, and I can see it’s made more mess than it fixed.\n\nLike with the garage work and some equipment — I went ahead and put deposits down without really sitting down with my wife and talking it through. In my mind, I was thinking, “If I don’t move on this now, we’ll miss the chance, or the work won’t get done, or it’ll cost more later.” It felt practical to me at the time, even responsible in a way. But I didn’t stop and think how it would feel to her, finding out money had already been committed and she hadn’t been part of the decision. So instead of getting something handled, I created a whole other problem at home.\n\nAnd the same thing happened with the driving back and forth between the houses. People were telling me I was doing too much, that I was taking chances, and I heard them, but I didn’t really take it in. I kept thinking, “It has to get done, and I’m the one who can do it.” Same at the plant — I was rushing jobs, pushing through, even after people warned me I was cutting it too close or taking risks. I thought I was being efficient. Looking back, I can see how it looked reckless.\n\nSo yes, if I’m being honest, my way of handling things has caused more problems than I meant it to. I still have this feeling like things won’t get done right unless I stay on top of them, but I can’t deny that I’ve caused trouble by charging ahead.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your attention to details and desire for things to be done a certain way interferes with your ability to complete tasks efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly.\n\nI mean, I do like things done correctly. I’ve worked around machinery and jobs where if somebody skips a step or does it sloppy, it can matter. So I’ve always paid attention to how things are done, and I can get irritated if people act like “close enough” is good enough. But I don’t think I’m sitting there polishing one tiny detail so long that nothing gets finished.\n\nIf anything, lately people would probably say I’m going too fast, not too slow. My problem hasn’t felt like getting stuck on details — it’s more that I see what needs doing and I want it handled, and I don’t have much patience for waiting around or rehashing it over and over.\n\nNow, maybe my wife would say I have a certain way I want things done and I push that too hard. I can see that. If I think a job should be done in a particular order, I’ll keep explaining it, and I may not leave much room for somebody else’s way. But in my mind that’s about being efficient, not being unable to finish.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that attention to details is keeping me from completing tasks efficiently. If there’s interference, it’s probably more in how I deal with people when I think something needs to get done a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior or the way you've been handling things lately is significantly impacting your daily life or relationships in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, it has. I wish I could say it was just people overreacting or not understanding what I was trying to do, but it’s gotten to the point where there have been real consequences.\n\nAt work, I ended up being pulled off after a safety incident. They’ve started stepping in or questioning me because they feel like things got out of hand. I don’t like being treated like I can’t make my own choices, but I also can’t pretend they’re making it up. No… I don’t think I’d use that word.\n\nI mean, there are things I want done properly. I’ll be in a great mood, joking around, feeling like I’ve got all the energy in the world, like I’m ten feet tall and can carry the whole thing myself. And then somebody — my wife, or my boy — asks one little question, something simple like why I’m doing it that way or whether it can wait, and I snap. I don’t always mean to, but it comes out like, “Either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of my way.”\n\nBecause in that moment it doesn’t feel like they’re helping. It feels like they’re slowing me down. And I know that probably sounds difficult to live with, but when I’m in it, I just feel so sure that my plan is the right one and everybody else needs to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Are your relationships with family or friends being affected by your behavior or the way you're handling things lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like admitting that, because in my head I’m just trying to keep things moving and make sure nobody drops the ball, but yes, it’s affected things.\n\nAt work, it got bad enough that they pulled me off after a safety incident. I was sure I had things under control, and I didn’t see it the way they saw it at the time, but afterward everybody acted like I couldn’t be trusted to make the right call. That was humiliating. I’ve worked hard my whole life, and then suddenly people are looking at me like I’m a problem.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and family have definitely changed how they deal with me. They don’t really trust me with certain decisions right now — money, driving, even parts of the move. They’ll say they’re just being careful, or that things have gotten out of hand, but it feels like they’re taking over. If I want to make a purchase or decide when we’re going somewhere or how the move should be handled, there’s this pause, this look between them, like they’ve already decided I’m not being reasonable.\n\nSo yes, my relationships are strained. My wife is tired, my family is wary around me, and I can feel that. I still think a lot of what I was trying to do needed doing, but I can’t really say it hasn’t caused trouble, because it has.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over, even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: Repeating thoughts or behaviors… I’m not sure I know exactly what you mean by that.\n\nI don’t think I have, like, a ritual or something I’m doing over and over because I can’t stop myself. I’m not counting things or checking a lock twenty times, if that’s what you’re asking. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nNow, I do get stuck on things in the sense that if something needs doing, I’ll keep coming back to it in my head, and I’ll talk about it more than other people probably want me to. My wife might say I repeat myself, because I’ll keep explaining why something has to be handled a certain way. But from my side, that feels more like trying to get the point across, not like some thought I’m trying to stop and can’t.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing, unless I’m misunderstanding the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of handling things is causing you more problems than it's solving?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to admit that it is. I don’t like saying that, because when I’m doing it, it feels like I’m solving the problem. It feels like I’m the one moving things along while everybody else is standing around talking it to death. But then afterward there’s a bill, or an argument, or somebody upset with me, and I can see it’s made more mess than it fixed.\n\nLike with the garage work and some equipment — I went ahead and put deposits down without really sitting down with my wife and talking it through. In my mind, I was thinking, “If I don’t move on this now, we’ll miss the chance, or the work won’t get done, or it’ll cost more later.” It felt practical to me at the time, even responsible in a way. But I didn’t stop and think how it would feel to her, finding out money had already been committed and she hadn’t been part of the decision. So instead of getting something handled, I created a whole other problem at home.\n\nAnd the same thing happened with the driving back and forth between the houses. People were telling me I was doing too much, that I was taking chances, and I heard them, but I didn’t really take it in. I kept thinking, “It has to get done, and I’m the one who can do it.” Same at the plant — I was rushing jobs, pushing through, even after people warned me I was cutting it too close or taking risks. I thought I was being efficient. Looking back, I can see how it looked reckless.\n\nSo yes, if I’m being honest, my way of handling things has caused more problems than I meant it to. I still have this feeling like things won’t get done right unless I stay on top of them, but I can’t deny that I’ve caused trouble by charging ahead.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your attention to details and desire for things to be done a certain way interferes with your ability to complete tasks efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly.\n\nI mean, I do like things done correctly. I’ve worked around machinery and jobs where if somebody skips a step or does it sloppy, it can matter. So I’ve always paid attention to how things are done, and I can get irritated if people act like “close enough” is good enough. But I don’t think I’m sitting there polishing one tiny detail so long that nothing gets finished.\n\nIf anything, lately people would probably say I’m going too fast, not too slow. My problem hasn’t felt like getting stuck on details — it’s more that I see what needs doing and I want it handled, and I don’t have much patience for waiting around or rehashing it over and over.\n\nNow, maybe my wife would say I have a certain way I want things done and I push that too hard. I can see that. If I think a job should be done in a particular order, I’ll keep explaining it, and I may not leave much room for somebody else’s way. But in my mind that’s about being efficient, not being unable to finish.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that attention to details is keeping me from completing tasks efficiently. If there’s interference, it’s probably more in how I deal with people when I think something needs to get done a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior or the way you've been handling things lately is significantly impacting your daily life or relationships in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, it has. I wish I could say it was just people overreacting or not understanding what I was trying to do, but it’s gotten to the point where there have been real consequences.\n\nAt work, I ended up being pulled off after a safety incident. That’s not a small thing. I’ve worked around equipment for years, so being told I couldn’t keep going because of how I was handling things — that hit me hard. In my head I thought I was just moving fast and getting things done, but other people saw it as unsafe, and apparently it was serious enough that they took me out of it.\n\nAnd at home it’s affected just about everything. My wife and family don’t really trust me right now with decisions — money decisions, driving back and forth, things about the move. They’ve started stepping in or questioning me because they feel like things got out of hand. I don’t like being treated like I can’t make my own choices, but I also can’t pretend they’re making it up. I did make decisions without talking them through, and I pushed ahead when people were telling me to slow down.\n\nSo yes, it’s impacted my daily life and relationships. It’s caused arguments, it’s made my family nervous around my judgment, and it’s affected my work. That’s hard for me to admit, but it’s true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things would fall apart or not meet your standards if you weren't in charge of them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do feel that way. Maybe more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up thinking, “I have to control everything.” I don’t see it that way in the moment. What I feel is that there are all these things that need doing, and if I don’t push them forward, they’re either not going to happen, or they’re going to be done halfway. The move, for example — there’s just so much involved, and I keep thinking, “Somebody has to keep this moving.” And with the new garage, I want it rebuilt right, not patched together or delayed until everyone forgets what needed to be done.\n\nAnd it’s not only at home. At church, if things are disorganized, I start seeing what needs to be arranged and who should be doing what, and I feel like I can help get it straightened out. Then at work, with the younger fellows, I find myself wanting to show them how the job ought to be done, because I’ve been doing this kind of work a long time and I know when someone is cutting corners or not thinking ahead.\n\nSo yes, I’m here because everyone says I’ve been doing too much, but from inside my own head it feels more like I’m the only one with enough drive to keep all these things from slipping. I know that probably sounds arrogant, and I don’t mean it that way. It just feels like if I step back, things won’t be handled the way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things have to be perfect, or as close to perfect as possible, for you to consider them done?\n\nPatient: Perfect? No… I don’t think I’d use that word.\n\nI mean, there are things I want done properly. If something is supposed to be level, safe, lined up, measured right — then yes, I don’t like calling it finished if it’s obviously wrong. That’s just how I was brought up and how I worked. In the kind of work I’ve done, you can’t just say, “Well, it’s mostly okay,” if mostly okay means somebody gets hurt or the machine doesn’t run right.\n\nBut “perfect” sounds like I’m standing there unable to move on because one little thing is off, and I don’t really see myself that way. I’m not saying every screw has to shine or every corner has to look brand new before I’ll accept it. I can live with things being good enough if they’re actually good enough.\n\nWhat bothers me is when people say something is done and I can tell they rushed it or skipped parts, or they didn’t think through what happens next. Then I have a hard time letting it go. I’ll keep pointing it out, and maybe I push too much, because in my mind I’m thinking, “If we’re doing it, why not do it right the first time?”\n\nSo… no, not perfect exactly. But I do have a pretty firm idea of what “done right” means, and I don’t always trust other people’s version of “done.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're superior to others or have a stronger sense of self-importance than those around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “superior.” That word makes me uncomfortable, honestly. I don’t walk around thinking I’m better than everybody else as a person, or that I’m more important in some grand way.\n\nBut… I can see how I might come across like that. Especially when I’m convinced I know the right way something should be handled. I’ve got a lot of years behind me, I’ve done hard work, I’ve had to learn by doing, and sometimes when someone younger or less experienced is making a decision, I catch myself thinking, “No, no, that’s not right — you’re missing the obvious thing here.” And I probably don’t always say that gently.\n\nSo if you mean, do I sometimes believe my judgment is better than other people’s in certain situations? Yes, probably. I do. I don’t think that’s the same as feeling superior overall, but maybe the people around me don’t feel the difference when I’m pushing my opinion. They just hear me acting like I know best.\n\nI guess I’d put it this way: I don’t feel like I’m above people, but I do have trouble stepping back when I think I understand something better than they do. And maybe that can look like self-importance from the outside.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. Uncomfortable, yes, sometimes. It depends what it is.

If I’ve pictured how something should go — not in some artistic way, just practically, like the steps that make sense — and then someone does it in a way that seems backwards or careless, I can feel myself getting tight inside. I’ll start thinking through all the problems that are going to come from it. Like, “Well, if you put that there, then later this won’t fit,” or “If you skip that now, someone’s going to have to redo the whole thing.” So I don’t know if that’s anxiety or irritation or just not being able to leave it alone.

I wouldn’t say everything has to be exactly as I imagined it. I can adjust if someone has a better way. I really can. But if their way looks wrong to me, I have trouble just standing there and saying, “Fine, whatever.” I’ll want to explain it, and then explain it again, and then maybe I’m still thinking about it afterward.

So… yes, I get uncomfortable when things are done in a way that doesn’t make sense to me. But I don’t know if it’s because it isn’t my vision exactly, or because I’m convinced there’s going to be a problem if it’s not handled right.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here mostly because my family and a few people around me keep saying I’ve been doing too much. But honestly, from where I’m standing, it doesn’t feel like “too much” so much as finally getting things moving the way they ought to be moved.\n\nWe’ve got this move to handle, and if I don’t keep after every box, every list, every little detail, it just sits there. And then there’s the new garage — I don’t want it thrown together sloppy, I want it rebuilt right, with a proper setup, the way a man can actually use it. On top of that I’ve been helping organize things at church, because somebody has to keep people on track there too. And even at work, I still try to show the younger fellows how the job ought to be done, because they rush or they miss steps, and I’ve been around long enough to know better.\n\nSo I guess that’s why I’m here. Everybody says I’m pushing too hard, taking on too much, but to me it feels like I’m the only one with enough drive to get it all handled.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're the only one who really knows how to get things done properly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, if I’m being honest. I don’t mean that to sound arrogant, but it’s just… when something needs doing, I can see all the pieces of it. Other people seem to look at one box or one errand or one little task and think that’s enough, but I’m looking at the whole thing.\n\nWith the move, for example, there are boxes, labels, what goes where, what has to be done first, what can’t be left until the last minute — and if I don’t keep pushing, it just drifts. Same with the garage. I don’t want some half-done setup where tools are piled in a corner and nothing makes sense. I want it rebuilt properly, laid out right, usable.\n\nAnd church too — people mean well, but somebody has to organize things or everyone just talks in circles. Then at work, I still try to show the younger fellows the right way, because they cut corners or don’t think ahead. So yes, I suppose I do feel like I’m the one with enough drive to actually get things done the way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your efforts to get everything done are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Stress or anxiety… I don’t know that I’d call it that, exactly. I mean, I get worked up when things aren’t being handled right, sure. Anybody would, if they saw things sliding or people standing around waiting for someone else to make a decision.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel to me like worry, like I’m sitting there scared or panicking. It’s more like there’s a lot to do and I can see it all, so I keep moving. Maybe other people look at that and say I’m stressed, but from my side it feels more like pressure to keep everything from turning into a mess.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it as anxiety. More like frustration when folks don’t keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things have to be done exactly your way in order for them to be done correctly?\n\nPatient: Well… “exactly my way” sounds a little harsher than I’d put it, but yes, I suppose there’s truth in that. It feels like I can already see how something ought to be done, start to finish, and when people start questioning every step or trying to do it some sideways way, I get… I get pretty impatient.\n\nLately it’s been more than just wanting things orderly. I’ll be in a great mood, joking around, feeling like I’ve got all the energy in the world, like I’m ten feet tall and can carry the whole thing myself. And then somebody — my wife, or my boy — asks one little question, something simple like why I’m doing it that way or whether it can wait, and I snap. I don’t always mean to, but it comes out like, “Either get on board with what I’m doing or get out of my way.”\n\nBecause in that moment it doesn’t feel like they’re helping. It feels like they’re slowing me down. And I know that probably sounds difficult to live with, but when I’m in it, I just feel so sure that my plan is the right one and everybody else needs to catch up.\n\nDoctor: Are your relationships with family or friends being affected by your behavior or the way you're handling things lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like admitting that, because in my head I’m just trying to keep things moving and make sure nobody drops the ball, but yes, it’s affected things.\n\nAt work, it got bad enough that they pulled me off after a safety incident. I was sure I had things under control, and I didn’t see it the way they saw it at the time, but afterward everybody acted like I couldn’t be trusted to make the right call. That was humiliating. I’ve worked hard my whole life, and then suddenly people are looking at me like I’m a problem.\n\nAnd at home… my wife and family have definitely changed how they deal with me. They don’t really trust me with certain decisions right now — money, driving, even parts of the move. They’ll say they’re just being careful, or that things have gotten out of hand, but it feels like they’re taking over. If I want to make a purchase or decide when we’re going somewhere or how the move should be handled, there’s this pause, this look between them, like they’ve already decided I’m not being reasonable.\n\nSo yes, my relationships are strained. My wife is tired, my family is wary around me, and I can feel that. I still think a lot of what I was trying to do needed doing, but I can’t really say it hasn’t caused trouble, because it has.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or behaviors over and over, even when you try to stop?\n\nPatient: Repeating thoughts or behaviors… I’m not sure I know exactly what you mean by that.\n\nI don’t think I have, like, a ritual or something I’m doing over and over because I can’t stop myself. I’m not counting things or checking a lock twenty times, if that’s what you’re asking. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nNow, I do get stuck on things in the sense that if something needs doing, I’ll keep coming back to it in my head, and I’ll talk about it more than other people probably want me to. My wife might say I repeat myself, because I’ll keep explaining why something has to be handled a certain way. But from my side, that feels more like trying to get the point across, not like some thought I’m trying to stop and can’t.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re describing, unless I’m misunderstanding the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of handling things is causing you more problems than it's solving?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to admit that it is. I don’t like saying that, because when I’m doing it, it feels like I’m solving the problem. It feels like I’m the one moving things along while everybody else is standing around talking it to death. But then afterward there’s a bill, or an argument, or somebody upset with me, and I can see it’s made more mess than it fixed.\n\nLike with the garage work and some equipment — I went ahead and put deposits down without really sitting down with my wife and talking it through. In my mind, I was thinking, “If I don’t move on this now, we’ll miss the chance, or the work won’t get done, or it’ll cost more later.” It felt practical to me at the time, even responsible in a way. But I didn’t stop and think how it would feel to her, finding out money had already been committed and she hadn’t been part of the decision. So instead of getting something handled, I created a whole other problem at home.\n\nAnd the same thing happened with the driving back and forth between the houses. People were telling me I was doing too much, that I was taking chances, and I heard them, but I didn’t really take it in. I kept thinking, “It has to get done, and I’m the one who can do it.” Same at the plant — I was rushing jobs, pushing through, even after people warned me I was cutting it too close or taking risks. I thought I was being efficient. Looking back, I can see how it looked reckless.\n\nSo yes, if I’m being honest, my way of handling things has caused more problems than I meant it to. I still have this feeling like things won’t get done right unless I stay on top of them, but I can’t deny that I’ve caused trouble by charging ahead.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your attention to details and desire for things to be done a certain way interferes with your ability to complete tasks efficiently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way exactly.\n\nI mean, I do like things done correctly. I’ve worked around machinery and jobs where if somebody skips a step or does it sloppy, it can matter. So I’ve always paid attention to how things are done, and I can get irritated if people act like “close enough” is good enough. But I don’t think I’m sitting there polishing one tiny detail so long that nothing gets finished.\n\nIf anything, lately people would probably say I’m going too fast, not too slow. My problem hasn’t felt like getting stuck on details — it’s more that I see what needs doing and I want it handled, and I don’t have much patience for waiting around or rehashing it over and over.\n\nNow, maybe my wife would say I have a certain way I want things done and I push that too hard. I can see that. If I think a job should be done in a particular order, I’ll keep explaining it, and I may not leave much room for somebody else’s way. But in my mind that’s about being efficient, not being unable to finish.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that attention to details is keeping me from completing tasks efficiently. If there’s interference, it’s probably more in how I deal with people when I think something needs to get done a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior or the way you've been handling things lately is significantly impacting your daily life or relationships in a negative way?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, it has. I wish I could say it was just people overreacting or not understanding what I was trying to do, but it’s gotten to the point where there have been real consequences.\n\nAt work, I ended up being pulled off after a safety incident. That’s not a small thing. I’ve worked around equipment for years, so being told I couldn’t keep going because of how I was handling things — that hit me hard. In my head I thought I was just moving fast and getting things done, but other people saw it as unsafe, and apparently it was serious enough that they took me out of it.\n\nAnd at home it’s affected just about everything. My wife and family don’t really trust me right now with decisions — money decisions, driving back and forth, things about the move. They’ve started stepping in or questioning me because they feel like things got out of hand. I don’t like being treated like I can’t make my own choices, but I also can’t pretend they’re making it up. I did make decisions without talking them through, and I pushed ahead when people were telling me to slow down.\n\nSo yes, it’s impacted my daily life and relationships. It’s caused arguments, it’s made my family nervous around my judgment, and it’s affected my work. That’s hard for me to admit, but it’s true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things would fall apart or not meet your standards if you weren't in charge of them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do feel that way. Maybe more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up thinking, “I have to control everything.” I don’t see it that way in the moment. What I feel is that there are all these things that need doing, and if I don’t push them forward, they’re either not going to happen, or they’re going to be done halfway. The move, for example — there’s just so much involved, and I keep thinking, “Somebody has to keep this moving.” And with the new garage, I want it rebuilt right, not patched together or delayed until everyone forgets what needed to be done.\n\nAnd it’s not only at home. At church, if things are disorganized, I start seeing what needs to be arranged and who should be doing what, and I feel like I can help get it straightened out. Then at work, with the younger fellows, I find myself wanting to show them how the job ought to be done, because I’ve been doing this kind of work a long time and I know when someone is cutting corners or not thinking ahead.\n\nSo yes, I’m here because everyone says I’ve been doing too much, but from inside my own head it feels more like I’m the only one with enough drive to keep all these things from slipping. I know that probably sounds arrogant, and I don’t mean it that way. It just feels like if I step back, things won’t be handled the way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things have to be perfect, or as close to perfect as possible, for you to consider them done?\n\nPatient: Perfect? No… I don’t think I’d use that word.\n\nI mean, there are things I want done properly. If something is supposed to be level, safe, lined up, measured right — then yes, I don’t like calling it finished if it’s obviously wrong. That’s just how I was brought up and how I worked. In the kind of work I’ve done, you can’t just say, “Well, it’s mostly okay,” if mostly okay means somebody gets hurt or the machine doesn’t run right.\n\nBut “perfect” sounds like I’m standing there unable to move on because one little thing is off, and I don’t really see myself that way. I’m not saying every screw has to shine or every corner has to look brand new before I’ll accept it. I can live with things being good enough if they’re actually good enough.\n\nWhat bothers me is when people say something is done and I can tell they rushed it or skipped parts, or they didn’t think through what happens next. Then I have a hard time letting it go. I’ll keep pointing it out, and maybe I push too much, because in my mind I’m thinking, “If we’re doing it, why not do it right the first time?”\n\nSo… no, not perfect exactly. But I do have a pretty firm idea of what “done right” means, and I don’t always trust other people’s version of “done.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're superior to others or have a stronger sense of self-importance than those around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “superior.” That word makes me uncomfortable, honestly. I don’t walk around thinking I’m better than everybody else as a person, or that I’m more important in some grand way.\n\nBut… I can see how I might come across like that. Especially when I’m convinced I know the right way something should be handled. I’ve got a lot of years behind me, I’ve done hard work, I’ve had to learn by doing, and sometimes when someone younger or less experienced is making a decision, I catch myself thinking, “No, no, that’s not right — you’re missing the obvious thing here.” And I probably don’t always say that gently.\n\nSo if you mean, do I sometimes believe my judgment is better than other people’s in certain situations? Yes, probably. I do. I don’t think that’s the same as feeling superior overall, but maybe the people around me don’t feel the difference when I’m pushing my opinion. They just hear me acting like I know best.\n\nI guess I’d put it this way: I don’t feel like I’m above people, but I do have trouble stepping back when I think I understand something better than they do. And maybe that can look like self-importance from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when things are not done exactly as you envisioned them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. Uncomfortable, yes, sometimes. It depends what it is.\n\nIf I’ve pictured how something should go — not in some artistic way, just practically, like the steps that make sense — and then someone does it in a way that seems backwards or careless, I can feel myself getting tight inside. I’ll start thinking through all the problems that are going to come from it. Like, “Well, if you put that there, then later this won’t fit,” or “If you skip that now, someone’s going to have to redo the whole thing.” So I don’t know if that’s anxiety or irritation or just not being able to leave it alone.\n\nI wouldn’t say everything has to be exactly as I imagined it. I can adjust if someone has a better way. I really can. But if their way looks wrong to me, I have trouble just standing there and saying, “Fine, whatever.” I’ll want to explain it, and then explain it again, and then maybe I’m still thinking about it afterward.\n\nSo… yes, I get uncomfortable when things are done in a way that doesn’t make sense to me. But I don’t know if it’s because it isn’t my vision exactly, or because I’m convinced there’s going to be a problem if it’s not handled right.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
